Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for fitting me in, I really appreciate your time. 

Um, well, I guess I'm here because... I just want to make sure I'm looking at things the right way, if that makes sense? It’s mostly been some stress regarding my wife. I know she says the late meetings and, well, these little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really try to be understanding—I certainly don't want to be a difficult husband or make a fuss over nothing. 

But... well, for the past year, things have just lined up too precisely to feel accidental. It almost feels like there’s a private signal in them, you know? Like they're meant to tell me something she won’t say directly.

Well, um... I really want to be open to it just being a coincidence. I'm not trying to be suspicious or anything, I just... I can't help but see the pattern. I've never felt like anyone else was trying to send me hidden messages or doing anything out of the ordinary. \n\nI really try to get along well with everyone, and I don't want you to think I'm just... suspicious of people in general, because I'm definitely not. I'm actually a very trusting person, usually. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? Like, maybe it helps that it's only happening in this one specific situation with my wife? Oh, no. No, not at all. Um, I've never thought she was cheating on me or anything like that. I know sometimes when husbands talk about their wives acting differently, that's the first thing people might assume, but... no, she's very loyal.

I'm just a regular guy who noticed some patterns with my wife, that's all. I'm very grounded in reality otherwise. \n\nI hope that reassures you a bit? I really don't want you to think I'm losing touch with things or anything like that. I'm doing my best to be completely honest with you, so I hope it's okay that my answer to that one is just a simple no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any concerns that your wife is unfaithful?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, not at all. Um, I've never thought she was cheating on me or anything like that. I really don't want to paint her in a bad light, you know? She's a good person, and I'm definitely not a jealous guy. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... well, I certainly don't want to sound like I think I have some kind of special ability or anything like that. I really don't. I suppose if someone else looked really, really closely, they might see the pattern too? 

But... well, I know my wife says the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really try to just accept that, I do. It's just that for the past year, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. It feels more like... like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. And since it's directed at me, I guess I'm just the one who naturally picks up on it. 

I hope I don't sound foolish or unreasonable explaining it that way. I'm just trying to be as honest as I can about how it feels to read them. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you a helpful answer.

And, um, the therapist was the one who gently suggested I might benefit from coming in for an individual assessment. Just to get an outside perspective, which makes total sense to me. \n\nI'm really trying to handle it the right way and not let it get out of hand. Does that sound like a reasonable way to manage things?\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that these messages are really meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really don't want to sound stubborn. And I know she says it's all just normal office stuff, and I genuinely try to accept that. I want to be a supportive husband, I really do. \n\nBut... I guess, if I'm being completely honest—and I know you need me to be honest so you can do your job—I'm... I'm fairly certain. It's just that, for the past year, everything has lined up too precisely to feel accidental. It really feels like there’s a private signal in them, you know? Like they are meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. \n\nI'm not trying to be difficult or make things hard for her by believing that, I promise. I just... I can't seem to unsee the pattern once it's there. Does that make sense? I hope I'm not coming across as too unreasonable for thinking that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any trouble keeping your thoughts or speech organized, like jumping from topic to topic in a way other people find hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, I don't think so. Um... I actually have to be very organized with my thoughts, especially since I help teach the undergraduate seminars. You really can't jump around too much or you lose the students completely, you know? So, I'm pretty careful about staying on topic. \n\nI... I hope I haven't been rambling today? I've been trying my best to answer your questions clearly and directly. Am I doing okay with that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you exactly what you need, so please stop me if I ever start going off track. But generally, no, nobody has ever mentioned having a hard time following me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe your wife is secretly sending you messages through these work patterns, or could it possibly just be a coincidence?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really want to be open to it just being a coincidence. I know she tells me it's just regular office stuff—the late meetings, the little changes in how she words her emails—and I try so hard to just take her word for it. I really don't want to be the kind of husband who makes a big deal out of nothing.\n\nBut... well, if I'm answering you honestly, and I know that's what I'm supposed to do here... it just doesn't feel like a coincidence. For the past year, it's just... it's all lined up so precisely. It feels like she's leaving these little private signals for me, you know? Like she's trying to communicate something to me that she just can't bring herself to say out loud. \n\nI know that probably sounds a bit strange when I say it like that... does it sound crazy to you? I'm not trying to be suspicious or anything, I just... I can't help but see the pattern. I hope you understand I'm really trying my best to handle it well.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any beliefs like this about other people or situations, or is it only about your wife?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, it's really just... just about her. Um, with everyone else—the other teaching assistants, the professors, my students—everything is completely normal. I've never felt like anyone else was trying to send me hidden messages or doing anything out of the ordinary. \n\nI really try to get along well with everyone, and I don't want you to think I'm just... suspicious of people in general, because I'm definitely not. I'm actually a very trusting person, usually. \n\nI hope that's a good sign? Like, maybe it helps that it's only happening in this one specific situation with my wife? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture here, so please let me know if that answers your question. I'm really trying to be as helpful as I can.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing this pattern in your wife’s messages?\n\nPatient: Well, um... let's see. I'm 48 now, and it really just started about a year ago. So I guess I would have been 47. \n\nIt was right around the time she started working outside the home again. Before that, everything was... well, it was perfectly fine. I just started noticing these little things in the past twelve months or so. \n\nI hope it's helpful to know that it hasn't been going on forever? Like, it's a relatively recent thing. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right timeline so you can understand the whole picture. Am I explaining that clearly enough?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other unusual experiences, like seeing things other people don’t see?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. Um, I've never had anything like that happen. I don't see things or hear things that aren't there. My vision and hearing are perfectly fine, I promise. \n\nI know sometimes people with... well, with serious problems might experience things like that, but that's really not me. I'm just a regular guy who noticed some patterns with my wife, that's all. I'm very grounded in reality otherwise. \n\nI hope that reassures you a bit? I really don't want you to think I'm losing touch with things or anything like that. I'm doing my best to be completely honest with you, so I hope it's okay that my answer to that one is just a simple no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any concerns that your wife is unfaithful?\n\nPatient: Oh, no. No, not at all. Um, I've never thought she was cheating on me or anything like that. I know sometimes when husbands talk about their wives acting differently, that's the first thing people might assume, but... no, she's very loyal. \n\nIt's really just about the messages. I really don't want to paint her in a bad light, you know? She's a good person, and I'm definitely not a jealous guy. I've always trusted her completely in that regard. \n\nI hope it helps to know that? Like, that it's not about jealousy or another man or anything typical like that. I just want to make sure I'm giving you an accurate picture of our marriage and that I'm not coming across as, well, possessive. Does that make sense? I really want to be sure I'm answering exactly what you're looking for.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing this belief about your wife’s messages?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... I think we might have just touched on that a little bit ago? I'm so sorry if I wasn't clear the first time, maybe I rambled and didn't explain it very well. Like I mentioned, I was 47. It's really only been happening for about a year. \n\nUnless... did you mean something different this time when you used the word 'belief'? I just want to make sure I'm answering exactly what you're asking and not missing your point. I really don't mind going over it again at all, I just want to be as helpful as I can. Am I doing okay with these questions? I hope I'm giving you what you need.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these messages are hidden in a way that only you could detect, or could anyone else see the same pattern if they looked closely?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... well, I certainly don't want to sound like I think I have some kind of special ability or anything like that. I really don't. I suppose if someone else looked really, really closely, they might see the pattern too? \n\nBut... well, I know my wife says the late meetings and those little changes in her work messages are just normal office stuff. And I really try to just accept that, I do. It's just that for the past year, they’ve lined up too precisely to feel accidental. It feels more like... like there’s a private signal in them, meant to tell me something she won’t say directly. And since it's directed at me, I guess I'm just the one who naturally picks up on it. \n\nI hope I don't sound foolish or unreasonable explaining it that way. I'm just trying to be as honest as I can about how it feels to read them. Does that make sense? I just want to make sure I'm giving you a helpful answer.\n\nDoctor: In the past year, have you also had a period of feeling unusually depressed or unusually energized with much less need for sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. Um, I haven't felt depressed at all. I mean, everyone has a down day here and there, right? But nothing unusual or, you know, clinical. And I definitely haven't had any of those times where I'm super energized or not needing sleep. I'm pretty boring when it comes to sleep, I try to get my full eight hours every night. \n\nI really try to keep myself on a healthy schedule. I wouldn't want you to think I'm unstable or having mood swings or anything like that. I just go to work, do my job as a TA, and come home. I try to be very steady. \n\nIs that the kind of thing you were looking for? I hope I'm answering these right. I just want to reassure you that my mood and energy are completely normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been a suspicious or mistrustful person throughout your life, even before this issue with your wife started?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. Um, I've always tried to be a very trusting person, really. I like to see the good in people and give them the benefit of the doubt. I mean, I'm a TA, so I work with students all the time, and you really have to be open and supportive with them, you know? \n\nI don't want you to get the impression that I'm some kind of paranoid guy who goes around suspecting everyone of things. That's really not me. Before this... this situation with the messages started, I never really worried about people hiding things from me or anything like that. My wife and I, we've always had a very normal, quiet life. \n\nI hope that doesn't make this whole thing sound even stranger. I just want to be clear that this isn't a personality trait of mine. I'm usually very easygoing. Does that help paint a better picture of how I normally am? I just want to make sure you know I'm a reasonable person.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like someone or something outside of you is controlling your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. Um, I've never felt anything like that. I know my own mind, and I'm very much in control of my own choices. I wouldn't want you to think I'm... well, that I'm losing touch with reality in that way. \n\nI just have my own thoughts, like anyone else. I go about my day, I make my own decisions at work and at home. There's no outside force or anything strange like that going on. I'm just a regular guy trying to figure out what's happening in my marriage. \n\nI hope I'm not coming across as defensive. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression. It's important to me that you see I'm still very grounded, you know? I really just want to understand what she's trying to tell me.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from your wife’s messages, do you believe that other people are also talking about or referring to you in special ways?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 48 / teaching_assistant / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no. No, definitely not. Um, I don't think anyone else is doing anything like that. It’s really just... well, just the situation at home with my wife. 

I promise I don't walk around thinking strangers or my students or colleagues are talking about me or sending me signals. That would be... well, that would be pretty concerning, wouldn't it? I really wouldn't want you to think I'm the sort of person who imagines things everywhere I go. 

I'm just a regular teaching assistant trying to do a good job and, you know, figure out my marriage. I don't pay attention to what other people are doing in that way, and they certainly aren't focused on me. I hope that makes sense? I just want to make sure you know I'm keeping things in perspective. Is that helpful?

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, goodness, no. No, definitely not. That sounds... well, that sounds like something out of a science fiction movie, doesn't it? I would never think anything like that.
